ACTION TIMING

0–4.5s — SHOT 1. Ron eats, and eats well. The knife goes through the meat in one clean stroke; the fork lifts the slice, turns it once on the gold, and carries it up out of the frame. The camera rises with it to his mouth: the piece goes in, the lips close, and he chews — slowly, thoroughly, the jaw working under the skin, the eyes half-closed with pleasure, a small hum of approval in the throat. He swallows. The right hand sets the knife's edge back to the meat. The camera keeps pulling back: his hands cutting the next slice, his chest, his shoulders, and by 4.5s his whole face is in the top of the frame, three-quarter, looking down at the plate with the contentment of a man at his own table.

4.5–7s — SHOT 1 continues. The pull-back opens to the medium shot: Ron at his desk in the green lamp light, the gold tray throwing warm light up under his face, the dark far wall behind him, the room dark around him; the doorway he faces is across the room from him, off the left edge of the frame. He lifts the rocks glass, drinks — the ice knocking — sets it down, picks up the fork again. At 6.5s, from the corridor in front of him, footsteps — boots on carpet, steady, coming straight toward him. Ron's knife keeps cutting; he does not look up. He knows the walk.

7–9s — SHOT 1 continues. Adam enters the frame from the left edge, close to the lens — a dark shape stepping into the lamp light, his back and right shoulder to the camera at three-quarter, coat straight, a light dust on the shoulders — and crosses to the front of the desk in three strides, straight toward Ron, who faces him with his eyes still on the plate; the black bag hangs from Adam's right fist by its knot, swinging by his knee, a shape the size of a basketball, and, without a word, without slowing, he swings it up and sets it down on the free end of the gold tray, right beside the steak, firmly: the tray rings, the glass rocks once and the ice knocks against the crystal, the cut slices shift a centimetre on the gold. The bag sits upright on the gold beside the steak and about its size, a taut black shape with its knot on top, filling that end of the tray, the plastic settling with a short crinkle. Adam's hand leaves it and drops to his side. Ron's knife freezes mid-cut. His eyes go to the black shape and stay on it.

9–11s — SHOT 2. Adam, close. He is not looking at the bag; he is looking down at Ron — at the old man's face over the black shape on the gold. The face is still and closed: the eyes steady on Ron, the mouth shut, the breath going out once, slowly, through the nose, the chest settling under the dusty coat. Nothing else moves. It is the face of a man who has put a thing on a table and is now watching what the other man will do with it — and already knows. A small tightening at the corner of the jaw is the only thing that changes in two seconds.

11–14.5s — SHOT 3. Ron, a piece still in his mouth, looks up from the bag to Adam and back to the bag. Then, with care, he lays the knife and the fork down side by side on the edge of the tray, blade and tines pointing the same way. He finishes the mouthful — one slow chew, a swallow — very surprised, the surprise of a man whose dinner has been interrupted by exactly what he ordered. Then the face opens: he sits back and begins to clap — slow, unhurried, five, six claps of the ringed hands — and the laugh comes up out of his chest with them, head tipping back. Over the last claps, warm and delighted: "Haa! Adam. Now every dog knows its place — eh? Eh?" — and he laughs again at his own line, the laugh going on a moment too long, the hands coming to rest on the desk either side of the tray.

14.5–17s — SHOT 4. Adam, three-quarter, looking down at the laughing man — at the neat cutlery, the gold, the ringed hands. He does not laugh. Contempt, held and cold: the lip does not move but the eyes go over Ron the way they would go over something on the pavement, and the breath goes in once through the nose. He says, quiet, level: "I'm done, Ron. I'm out." Then he turns — a clean turn away from the desk, no flourish, hands at his sides — and walks back toward the door he came in by, his back filling the frame as he goes: stride one.

17–19.5s — SHOT 5, both men in frame, the camera looking across the room toward the door. Ron's laugh dies in his throat. For half a second the face is still shaped for laughing and the eyes are not — and then a small panic moves through him: he comes up out of the chair, both hands planting on the desk either side of the tray, the chair rolling back behind him, leaning after Adam's back, his face in three-quarter profile toward the door, and he says it — not a shout, a man who has lost the thread: "Out? What do you mean — out?" And in the far half of the frame Adam walks away toward the door: stride two, stride three under "Out?", stride four under "mean", the same length of step, the same speed, the shoulders level, the arms swinging easy at his sides, the head never turning, the door three strides ahead of him now. He is a man walking to a door, and that is all he is.

19.5–23.5s — SHOT 6, Ron alone, the push-in. The camera begins to creep in on him from the medium shot — slow, even, unstoppable, the frame tightening a little with every half second. His face, lit from below by the lamp and the gold, his eyes on the door off frame-left — on the back that is still moving away from him. Off-screen, steady, Adam's boots: stride five at 20s, stride six at 21s, the same tempo. Ron says nothing yet. 19.5–20.5s, the watching: the panic in the eyes, the mouth a little open, the question doing nothing, the camera already closing. 20.5–21s, the recognition: the eyes go still and sharpen — one beat, not a search: he has the one thing, and he knows it will work; the jaw sets with it. 21–21.5s, the breath: in through the nose, the chin lifting a centimetre, the lips parting, the frame now at his collar. 21.5–23s, the line, quiet, almost gentle, to the back across the room: "Your father's back." — the camera still creeping in through the words, the frame closing on the face, and on "back" it arrives: a tight close-up, the eyes large, the mouth just closed on the word. And on "back", off-screen, the seventh footfall lands — and after it, nothing. The boots have stopped. 23–23.5s: the camera holds on the tight face, Ron's eyes on the door, listening to the silence he has made.

23.5–30s — SHOT 7. Adam from ahead at three-quarter, his face turned a fraction toward his left shoulder, filling the left half of the frame, the dark door frame standing untouched at the right edge half a metre ahead of him, Ron small and soft across the room behind him, still leaning on the desk. Adam is standing still — the shot opens on the last of the stop: the trailing boot already set down beside the other, two boots side by side on the floor half a stride short of the threshold, the coat hem swinging once and coming to rest, the shoulders settling, the hands hanging open at his sides, touching nothing. The face is toward the dark corridor ahead, and the news lands on it in stages, and the camera watches each one. First the surprise, 23.5–25s: the eyes widen a fraction, the brow lifts, the breath is held, the eyes fixed on nothing in the dark ahead. Then the anger, slowly, 25–29.5s — it climbs into the face one piece at a time: the jaw tightens first; then the nostrils; then the eyes narrow again and go darker than before; the head lowers a centimetre; at his sides the open hands close slowly into fists and stay closed. Four and a half seconds of a man doing arithmetic he does not like. Behind him the soft shape of Ron slowly straightens off the desk. At 29.5s Adam's shoulders begin to come round toward Ron, the head starting to follow — and on the turn, before the face has finished coming round, HARD CUT to black.

SCENE DIRECTION (shared, unspoken — both men play it): close the account and part on the terms we always had. Ron plays it as the man who dines and praises; Adam plays it as the man who delivers and goes. Both want the transaction finished. The event is a leash being put back on a man who has just taken it off: it ends on the one word that does it — "father" — and everything before it is two men finding out that the old words no longer work on him.

ACTING TASK — RON (fully invested; the work is in his hands, then in his eyes, and in shot 6 in the eyes and the mouth alone, while the camera comes for him):

MOTIVE (his fuel): Adam is the best thing he owns, and tonight proved it; he is going to enjoy that — the dinner first, then the dog.

GOAL: eat well, praise the man, and keep the man — the way he always has.

OBSTACLE: the man says he is leaving, and Ron has never had a plan for that, because it has never happened.

TACTIC: the dinner is the pleasure of a job well done before the job has come through the door, and he eats it the way he does everything — properly, in control, every slice the same size. He hears the boots coming straight at him across the room and does not look up, because looking up would mean he was waiting, and a man does not look up for a man he owns. The bag surprises him only in its timing; the surprise is real, and so is the care with which he lays the knife and fork down and finishes his mouthful — he will not be untidy even for this. The applause is real: he is proud of his dog and says so in the only language he has. When Adam says he is done, Ron's first move is bewilderment — the question, the body coming up out of the chair — and he watches the back keep walking while he asks it. Then, alone in the frame with the camera closing on him, he watches the question fail — and the answer is already there; he does not have to look for it, he has been sitting on it for years. Recognition, not deliberation: one beat, and he knows it will work. He says it quietly, to the back, because a quiet word that works is worth more to him than a loud one that does not — and when the boots stop he does not smile; he listens to the silence, because now he has to see what he has done.

Moment to moment:

— the eating — eyes half-closed on the plate; the knife and fork know the work; the hum

— the boots — coming at him head-on; the knife keeps cutting; the eyes stay on the food

— the bag — the knife freezes mid-cut; the eyes on the black shape beside his steak and they stay there

— the look up — bag, Adam, bag; the cutlery laid down with care

— the mouthful — one chew, a swallow; he finishes it because a man finishes his mouthful; the eyes already going up to Adam

— the applause — the eyes on Adam's face, looking for the pride to come back; it does not come back, and he laughs anyway

— "eh? Eh?" — the eyes checking both of Adam's eyes for the old agreement

— "I'm out" — the laugh dies while the face is still shaped for it; a small panic behind the eyes

— "Out? What do you mean — out?" — the body coming up, hands on the desk, the eyes on the back of Adam's head, watching the back move away with every word

— the push-in, watching — the eyes on the door, the mouth a little open; the boots going on; the frame closing on him

— the push-in, the recognition — the eyes go still and sharpen; one beat; the jaw sets; he has it

— the push-in, the breath — in through the nose; the chin lifts; the lips part

— "Your father's back" — quiet, to the back across the room; the eyes on the back of Adam's head through every word; the mouth closes on the last word as the frame arrives

— the silence — the boots have stopped; he listens; the eyes stay on the door; the face does not move

(Safety: eyes engaged in the task throughout — the plate, the bag, Adam's face, Adam's back, the door — never glassy; natural blink cadence; in the push-in every change is in the eyes and the mouth, small, and the face holds its three-quarter to the camera as the frame tightens.)

ACTING TASK — ADAM (fully invested; the work is in his eyes and in his walk):

MOTIVE (his fuel): he has just carried a head down a corridor for this man, and found him at dinner with a napkin at his collar, and the man called him a dog for it. Again.

GOAL: put the bag down, hear nothing, and be through that door.

OBSTACLE: Ron's laugh, which wants him to stay and be praised; Ron's question, which wants an answer; and then the one word he has no defence against.

TACTIC: he gives Ron nothing — no word at the door, no pause at the desk, the bag set down on the tray on purpose, beside the food. In the close-up he does not look at the bag — he has carried it, he knows what it is — he looks at the old man looking at it, and waits, and what he sees coming is exactly what comes. The contempt after the applause is the only thing he allows to show, and he holds it rather than acting on it. The line is flat because anything louder would be a conversation. The turn is clean, and from the turn on he is walking — that is his whole task: seven strides back to the door he came in by, at one speed, eyes on the door, hands loose at his sides, Ron's voice behind him as much a part of the room as the lamp. The question gets nothing: the walk is the answer. The word about his father is different — it is not Ron's, it is his — and it arrives as the seventh stride lands, and the feet come together, and the walk is over, and he does not reach for anything, because a man who reaches for a door frame is a man who needed holding up. He stands on his own two feet and takes it. The surprise is a door he thought was closed. The anger comes slowly because it has to travel through everything that door means before it reaches his face — and once it is there, it turns him round, because now there is something in this room he has to look at.

Moment to moment:

— the walk in — straight at the desk, eyes on the tray, not the man; the bag swinging from his fist; it goes down beside the meat on purpose; the hand drops to his side

— the close-up — eyes down on Ron's face, not the bag; the breath going out slowly; the jaw tightening a fraction; waiting for the thing he knows is coming

— the cutlery, the applause — he watches the hands lay the knife down, then the mouth; the contempt is in the eyes and the held breath

— "eh? Eh?" — he lets it land; he does not answer it

— "I'm done, Ron. I'm out." — the eyes on Ron's eyes for the whole line, then off him with the turn

— stride one — eyes on the door; the pace set; arms easy

— "Out? What do you mean — out?" — strides two, three, four, the same length, the same speed; the head does not turn; the eyes stay on the door; the question is air

— strides five, six, seven — off-screen; heard; the same tempo; the seventh lands on the last word and after it nothing

— the settle — the coat comes to rest, the shoulders settle, the hands hang open; standing still is the first answer

— the surprise — the eyes open a fraction, the breath held; a door he thought was closed

— the anger, slowly — the jaw, then the nostrils, then the eyes narrowing darker; the head coming down a centimetre; the hands at his sides closing into fists

— the turn begins — shoulders first, then the head, toward Ron; he is going to look at the man who said it — and the cut takes him mid-turn

(Safety: eyes engaged on real targets throughout — the tray, Ron's face, Ron, the door, the dark corridor, then Ron again — never glassy; the flatness is control, not absence.)

PHYSICS

The steak is a real slab on a long bone, seared dark brown all over, dry-seared, the cut faces firm and brown; the knife goes through it in one stroke with a little resistance at the crust; the fork lifts a slice that bends slightly under its own weight; chewing is slow and closed-mouthed, the jaw visible under the skin. The bag is dense — about five kilos in a basketball-sized taut black plastic bag — and hangs straight down from Adam's fist by its knot, swinging a little by his knee with his stride; it swings up with real mass and lands upright on the gold with a firm dull thump and stays upright and knotted, about the size of the steak beside it, the plastic settling with a short crinkle. When it lands the gold tray rings, the glass rocks once on its base and the ice knocks against the crystal, the whisky tilts inside the glass and settles, the cut slices shift a centimetre on the gold; the tray, the steak and the cloth around it stay exactly as they were, dry. Knife and fork laid down together click once on the gold. Ron's chair rolls back when he rises; his palms land flat on the desk. The push-in of shot 6 is a dolly creeping forward on a straight line at one slow speed, the face growing in the frame evenly, the focus riding with it. Adam's coat hangs straight and moves as heavy cloth on a walking man, the hem swinging with the stride and settling when he stands; the dust on his shoulders is a dull matte film that takes the lamp light differently from the clean cloth below it. Adam's walk from the turn is seven strides of one length at one tempo — a metronome — back across the room toward the door he came in by, through Ron's question, through Ron's silence, and through Ron's line, arms swinging easy at his sides; the seventh footfall lands on "back" and the trailing boot sets down beside it, and shot 7 opens on the settle — two boots side by side, the body's momentum going down through them, the coat hem swinging once and coming to rest — half a stride short of the threshold, with open floor around him and the door frame half a metre ahead of his shoulder; his hands hang at his sides and slowly close into fists as the anger builds; the turn begins in the shoulders, the head following, cut before it completes.

LIGHTING

Practical only, as @loc_room_ron_v1: the green-shaded desk lamp at Ron's left is the key — warm light on the gold tray, which throws it back up under Ron's face and onto the meat, the knife blade, the glass, the white napkin at his collar and the matte-black bag; in the close-up of shot 2 the same under-light from the gold reaches up to Adam's face from below and to one side, warm on the jaw and the cheekbone, the eyes catching it, the rest of his face and the room behind him falling to dark; in the push-in of shot 6 it is the whole light on Ron's face, warm from below, growing on the face as the frame tightens, the eyes catching it, the green shade sliding out of the frame as the camera closes. The rest of the room falls to shadow, the walls barely there; the doorway across the room from Ron, in front of him, is a dark rectangle with the darker corridor beyond, and Adam steps out of that dark into the lamp light when he enters. Walking back to the door Adam moves into shadow, his face edged by the lamp behind him, and where he stands in shot 7 the lamp catches the near side of his clean face and the dust on his near shoulder, the dark door frame a vertical shadow at the right edge of the picture, the corridor black beyond it.

AUDIO

No music. Room tone: a quiet office, a clock somewhere, the faint hum of the lamp. Eating — neat, unhurried: the knife through the crust and onto the gold with a small scrape, the fork's tines, slow closed chewing, a hum of approval, a swallow, ice knocking in the glass. At 6.5s, boots on the corridor carpet, steady, growing, coming toward the room, then on the office floor; the bag swinging on plastic. The bag: a swing, a firm dull thump on the gold tray — the tray ringing, the glass rocking once, the ice knocking against the crystal — the plastic settling. In the close-up of Adam: only the room tone, and one slow breath out through his nose. Knife and fork laid down together with one click. One chew, a swallow. Ron's applause: six slow claps. Ron's laugh: deep, from the chest, head back, going on a moment too long.

[Dialogue — English, native lip sync ]

12–14.5s — RON, warm, delighted, over the last claps: "Haa! Adam. Now every dog knows its place — eh? Eh?" — then the laugh.

15–16.5s — ADAM, quiet, level: "I'm done, Ron. I'm out."

17.5–19s — RON, the laugh cut off, bewildered, a small panic under it, no shout: "Out? What do you mean — out?"

19–21.5s — silence from Ron: two and a half seconds of nothing but Adam's boots at one steady tempo — strides five and six off-screen under the push-in — the lamp hum, the clock, Ron's short breath, and at 21.2s one breath drawn in through his nose.

21.5–23s — RON, quiet, almost gentle, to Adam's back across the room, on camera in the tightening close-up: "Your father's back."

Nothing else is said. The chair rolling back, palms landing flat on the desk. Adam's boots — seven strides at one steady tempo from the turn, three of them landing under Ron's question, two under Ron's silence, the seventh landing on the word "back" — and then one more sound: the trailing boot setting down flat beside the first, and after it nothing. Silence over the end of shot 6 and all of shot 7: the lamp hum, the clock, Ron's breath, Adam's breath held and then coming back slowly through the nose, the faint creak of leather as the hands close. Hard cut to black.

STYLE

Neo-noir: one green-shaded lamp in a black room, gold and seared meat and white linen against deep shadow, heavy natural grain, anamorphic ovals on the lamp and stretched highlights on the tray, the blade, the glass and the rings, shallow focus that puts one man sharp and the other soft. Quiet and slow, the pull-back unhurried, the push-in unhurried and tense, the cuts clean — the scene is one man dining, a man walking straight at him out of the dark, a black bag set down beside a steak, a young face waiting over it, an old face opening into laughter, a long walk back to a door, the camera creeping onto an old face as it finds the one word and says it, and a young face standing still in the dark, slowly turning to anger.

QUALITY

Ron's face and hands stable and matching the reference through the pull-back and through the whole push-in, the face holding its three-quarter to the camera as the frame tightens, knife in the right hand and fork in the left in every frame they are held; the steak, the bone, the gold tray, the glass and the cutlery consistent objects; the bag one consistent object — basketball-sized, about the size of the steak beside it, matte black, knotted, upright on the free end of the tray from 8s to the end; the whisky inside its glass in every frame; clean English lip sync on all four lines in the old man's voice and Adam's, Ron's last line synced on camera through the end of the push-in; Adam's face clean and unmarked and stable across shots 2, 4 and 7, his coat straight with a light dust on the shoulders in every shot, his hands empty and at his sides from the moment the bag is set down; Adam entering shot 1 from the left edge of the frame in the foreground and walking toward Ron, and leaving in shots 4 and 5 back the same way, away from Ron toward the door in front of him; Adam's seven strides unbroken and unchanged in tempo through Ron's question in shot 5 and audibly through shot 6, the seventh footfall landing exactly on the word "back"; shot 7 opening on Adam already standing, the coat settling, the door frame untouched at the right edge; the stillness of Adam's face in shot 2 with the one slow breath and the small tightening of the jaw readable; the beats on Ron's face through the push-in — watching, recognition, the breath, the line, the listening — distinct and readable in the eyes and mouth; the three stages on Adam's face at the end — surprise, the slow anger, the turn beginning — distinct and readable; consistent frame rate, no ghosting.

POSITIVE CONSTRAINTS

Two people in the scene: Ron at the desk, Adam arriving and leaving. Ron sits facing the door across the room; the door is in front of him for the whole scene. Every shot is a three-quarter angle — the camera off-axis to whichever face it is on — from the first frame to the last, including the whole of the push-in. Seven shots. Ron eats with a knife in his right hand and a fork in his left, neatly, mouth closed when he chews. The steak is dry-seared, dark brown outside with firm brown cut faces. The only liquid in the scene is the whisky, and it stays inside its glass from the first frame to the last — the glass rocks and the ice knocks when the bag lands, and the whisky settles inside it. The bag is the size of a basketball, about as big as the steak on its bone beside it, filling the free end of the tray — about a third of the gold — and standing about as tall as the rocks glass and a half; it hangs from Adam's fist by its knot; it is matte black, tied with one knot at the top, dry and clean on every side; it lands upright on the free end of the tray beside the steak at 8s and stays on the tray, upright and knotted, for the rest of the scene; the tray and the steak stay dry and clean beside it. Shot 1 is one continuous slow pull-back from an extreme close-up on Ron's hands and knife over the gold tray, past his mouth as he chews, to a medium shot of him at the desk, 0–9s, with the footsteps heard at 6.5s coming toward him, Adam entering the frame from the left edge in the foreground at 7s and walking to the front of the desk face to face with Ron, the bag set down at 8s, and Ron's knife freezing and his eyes going to the bag at 8–9s. Shot 2 at 9–11s is a close-up of Adam alone, still, his eyes down on Ron off-frame, one slow breath out, the jaw tightening a fraction. Shot 3 at 11–14.5s: Ron lays the knife and fork down, finishes his mouthful, applauds and laughs and speaks his first line. Adam speaks his one line at 15–16.5s, turns away from the desk and walks back toward the door he came in by with his hands at his sides. Shot 5 at 17–19.5s holds both men, looking across the room toward the door: Ron rises and asks "Out? What do you mean — out?" while Adam, in the same frame, walks away toward the door — strides two, three and four at one unchanged tempo, head never turning. Shot 6 at 19.5–23.5s is Ron alone with Adam off-screen and his boots heard continuing at the same tempo: one continuous slow push-in from a medium shot to a tight three-quarter close-up of Ron's face; he watches, recognition arrives in one beat, he draws a breath, and he speaks "Your father's back" at 21.5–23s on camera, the push-in arriving at the tight close-up on the word "back", the seventh footfall landing off-screen on that word and the boots falling silent; the shot holds half a second on his face listening. Shot 7 at 23.5–30s: Adam from ahead at three-quarter with Ron soft across the room behind him, already standing still half a stride short of the threshold, the coat settling, hands at his sides, touching nothing, the door frame standing untouched at the right edge — that is where his walk has ended, on Ron's last word and on nothing before it. At 23.5–25s surprise; at 25–29.5s anger climbing slowly into his face while his hands at his sides close into fists; at 29.5s his shoulders begin to turn toward Ron; hard cut to black mid-turn at 30s. Exactly four lines of dialogue, in English, exactly as written; Ron's voice is the old man's established voice; no other words; two and a half seconds of Ron's silence between his question and his last line. Adam's face is clean and unmarked, his hair as on the reference, his coat straight and hanging as on the reference with only a light dust on the shoulders, sleeves and boot toes. Adam carries only the bag, by its knot; it is set on the tray and stays there; from then on his hands are empty and stay at his sides.
